The base change $\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{Y,y})\to\mathrm{Y}$ not changing the fact that $f$ is \'etale, separated, and birational, one can restrict to the case where $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$, with $\mathrm{A}=\sh{O}_{Y,y}$.
\oldpage[IV-4]{167}
Let $\mathrm{A}'={}^{\mathrm{hs}}\mathrm{A}$, which is a local ring and an $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$-module faithful flat.
Set $\mathrm{Y}'=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A}')$, $f'=f_{(\mathrm{Y}')}:\mathrm{X}'\to\mathrm{Y}'$, so that $f'$ is \'etale and separated; moreover, as the morphism $\mathrm{Y}'\to\mathrm{Y}$ is flat, the reasoning of \sref{IV.6.15.4.1} shows that $f'$ is also birational.

Suppose therefore $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$, $\mathrm{A}$ strictly local, $f$ separated, \'etale and birational, and let us show that $f^{-1}(y)$ can contain only a single point.
Indeed, if $f^{-1}(y)$ contained two distinct points $x_1$, $x_2$, there would exist two $\mathrm{Y}$-sections $u_1$, $u_2$ of $f$ such that $u_1(y)=x_1$ and $u_2(y)=x_2$ \sref{IV.18.8.1}; but since $f$ is \'etale and separated, $u_1$ and $u_2$ would be isomorphisms of $\mathrm{Y}$ onto two connected components (open) of $\mathrm{X}$ \sref{IV.17.9.4} and there would then be two maximal points of $\mathrm{X}$ at least above every maximal point of $\mathrm{Y}$, which contradicts the hypothesis that $f$ is \emph{birational}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[18.10.19]
\label{IV.18.10.19}
Let $\mathrm{Y}$ be a reduced prescheme whose set of irreducible components is locally finite, $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ a separated morphism of finite type and formally unramified, $g$ a $\mathrm{Y}$-rational section of $f$ (i.e.\ a $\mathrm{Y}$-rational map from $\mathrm{Y}$ to $\mathrm{X}$), $\mathrm{U}$ the domain of definition of $g$ \sref[I]{I.7.2.1}, and let $\mathrm{Z}$ be the closure of $g(\mathrm{U})$ in $\mathrm{X}$.
Then, for all points $y\in\mathrm{Y}-\mathrm{U}$ such that $y$ is geometrically unibranch, $\mathrm{Z}_y=\mathrm{Z}\cap f^{-1}(y)=\varnothing$.
In particular, if $\mathrm{Y}$ is geometrically unibranch, $g(\mathrm{U})$ is a subset of $\mathrm{X}$ that is both open and closed.
If, for every closed irreducible $\mathrm{T}$ of $\mathrm{X}$, $f(\mathrm{T})$ is closed in $\mathrm{Y}$, $g$ is defined at every geometrically unibranch point of $\mathrm{Y}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $\mathrm{Y}$ is reduced and $f$ separated, it follows from \sref[I]{I.7.2.2} that there exists a $\mathrm{U}$-section $u$ of $f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})$ belonging to the class $g$; by \sref{IV.17.4.1.2}, moreover, $f$ is formally unramified, hence $u$ is an isomorphism of $\mathrm{U}$ onto the prescheme induced on the open set $u(\mathrm{U})$ of $\mathrm{X}$.
If one denotes also by $\mathrm{Z}$ the reduced sub-prescheme of $\mathrm{X}$ having $\mathrm{Z}$ for underlying space, $g(\mathrm{U})=u(\mathrm{U})$, being reduced, is induced also by $\mathrm{Z}$, and by construction $f'=f|\mathrm{Z}$ is a \emph{birational} morphism of $\mathrm{Z}$ to $\mathrm{Y}$, moreover formally unramified since $f$ is \sref{IV.17.1.3}.
The question being local over $\mathrm{Y}$ at the point $y$, one can restrict (replacing $\mathrm{Y}$ by a neighbourhood of $y$) to the case where $\mathrm{Y}$ is \emph{integral}; then $u(\mathrm{U})$ is irreducible, hence also $\mathrm{Z}$, and by construction $\mathrm{Z}$ is also integral.
Then \sref[I]{I.8.2.7} since $f'$ is dominant, the homomorphism $\sh{O}_{Y,y}\to\sh{O}_{Z,x}$ is injective for all $x\in\mathrm{Z}_y$, and one concludes from \sref{IV.18.10.2},~(iii)) that $f'$ is an \'etale isomorphism local at the point $x$; but this would imply that the $\mathrm{U}$-section $u$ would be extendable to an open set distinct from $\mathrm{U}$, contrary to the definition of the latter.
We therefore necessarily have $\mathrm{Z}_y=\varnothing$, which establishes the first assertion; the second is an obvious consequence.
Finally, under the last hypotheses of the statement, as $\mathrm{Z}$ is closed and irreducible, $f(\mathrm{Z})$ is closed in $\mathrm{Y}$, hence equal to $\mathrm{Y}$, i.e.\ $\mathrm{Z}_y\neq\varnothing$ for all $y\in\mathrm{Y}$, which finishes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[18.10.20]
\label{IV.18.10.20}
Let $\mathrm{Y}$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme.
One says that a morphism $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ is \emph{essentially proper} if it is locally of finite type and if, for every base change $\mathrm{Y}''\to\mathrm{Y}$, where $\mathrm{Y}''$ is \emph{locally Noetherian}, the image by $f_{(\mathrm{Y}'')}:\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{Y}'')}\to\mathrm{Y}''$ of every closed irreducible subset of $\mathrm{X}''$ is \emph{closed}.
\oldpage[IV-4]{168}
To say that $f$ is essentially proper for every $\mathrm{Y}$-scheme $\mathrm{Y}'=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ where $\mathrm{A}$ is a discrete valuation ring, the canonical map \sref[II]{II.7.3.2.2} relative to the $\mathrm{Y}'$-prescheme $\mathrm{X}\times_{\mathrm{Y}}\mathrm{Y}'$, is bijective.
The reasoning of \sref[II]{II.7.3.8} proves that this is the same as saying that $f$ (supposed locally of finite type) is \emph{separated} and that for every base change $\mathrm{Y}''\to\mathrm{Y}$, where $\mathrm{Y}''$ is \emph{locally Noetherian}, the image by $f_{(\mathrm{Y}'')}: \mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{Y}'')}\to\mathrm{Y}''$ of every closed irreducible subset of $\mathrm{X}''$ is \emph{closed}.
To say that $f$ is \emph{proper} (for $\mathrm{Y}$ locally Noetherian) means therefore that $f$ is \emph{essentially proper and of finite type} \sref[II]{II.7.3.8}; but one encounters important examples of essentially proper morphisms that are not of finite type (for example certain ``Picard preschemes'' or certain ``preschemes of N\'eron-Severi'' (chap.~VI)).
It evidently follows from \sref{IV.18.10.19} that if $\mathrm{Y}$ is \emph{locally Noetherian, reduced and geometrically unibranch}, and if $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ is \emph{essentially proper and not ramified}, then every $\mathrm{Y}$-rational section of $f$ is \emph{defined everywhere}.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[18.10.21]
\label{IV.18.10.21}
Let $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ be a locally of finite presentation morphism, $x$ a point of $\mathrm{X}$, $y=f(x)$, and suppose the ring $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$ integral and geometrically unibranch.
Let $r$ be the minimum number of generators of the $\sh{O}_{X,x}$-module $(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{Y}})_x$ (equal to the rank of the $k(x)$-vector space $(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{Y}})_x\otimes_{\sh{O}_{X,x}}k(x)$).
Then, for $f$ to be smooth at the point $x$, it is necessary and sufficient that, if $y'$ is the generic point of the unique irreducible component of $\mathrm{Y}$ containing $y$, there exists a generisation $x'$ of $x$ such that $f(x')=y'$ and $\dim_{x'}f^{-1}(y')\geq r$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If $f$ is smooth at the point $x$, it is also so in an open neighbourhood $\mathrm{U}$ of $x$, hence in every generisation $x'$ of $x$, and $\dim_{x'}(f^{-1}(f(x')))=r$ in each of these points, by virtue of \sref{IV.17.10.2}.
As $f$ is moreover flat in $\mathrm{U}$, there exists a generisation of $x$ above every generisation of $y$ \sref{IV.2.3.4}, which proves that the condition is necessary.
To show that it is sufficient, let us first note that by \sref{IV.17.5.1}, it suffices to prove that the morphism deduced from $f$ by the base change $\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{Y,y})\to\mathrm{Y}$ is smooth at the point $x$; one can therefore suppose that $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{Y,y})$.

The question is local on $\mathrm{X}$, hence \sref{IV.16.4.22} and \sref[0]{0.5.2.2}) one can suppose that $\mathrm{X}$ is affine, and that there exist $r$ sections $s_i$ ($1\leq i\leq r$) of $\sh{O}_X$ above $\mathrm{X}$ such that the sections $d_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{Y}}(s_i)$ generate $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{Y}}$.
Let
\[
  g:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{Y}[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_r]=\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{Y}}
\]
be the $\mathrm{Y}$-morphism corresponding to the homomorphism $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{Y}}^r\to f_*(\sh{O}_X)$ of $\sh{O}_Y$-Modules defined by the $s_i$ (considered as sections of $f_*(\sh{O}_X)$ above $\mathrm{Y}$) \sref[II]{II.1.2.7}.
We have the exact sequence
\[
  g^*(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{Z}/\mathrm{Y}})\to\Omega^1_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{Y}}\to\Omega^1_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{Z}}\to 0.
\]
As the $d\mathrm{T}_i$ ($1\leq i\leq r$) generate $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{Z}/\mathrm{Y}}$, it follows from the definition of $g$ that the homomorphism $g^*(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{Z}/\mathrm{Y}})\to\Omega^1_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{Y}}$ is surjective, hence $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{X}/\mathrm{Z}}=0$, and $g$ is consequently \emph{formally unramified} \sref{IV.17.4.1}.
We shall now see that $g$ is \emph{\'etale} at the point $x$, and taking into account \sref{IV.17.3.8}, this will prove that $f$ is smooth at the point $x$.
As $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$ is supposed integral and geometrically
\oldpage[IV-4]{169}
unibranch, so is $\sh{O}_{Z,z}$ by \sref{IV.17.5.7} where $z=g(x)$, since the structural morphism $\mathrm{Z}\to\mathrm{Y}$ is smooth \sref{IV.17.3.8}.
By virtue of \sref{IV.18.10.1}, it suffices to show that the homomorphism $\sh{O}_{Z,z}\to\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is injective; it amounts to the same thing to see that the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{X,x})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{Z,z})$ corresponding is \emph{dominant}, since $\sh{O}_{Z,z}$ is integral \sref[I]{I.1.2.7}.
Let $y'$ be the generic point of $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{Y,y})$ and let $z'$ be the generic point of $\mathrm{Z}=\operatorname{Spec}(\sh{O}_{Y,y}[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_r])$ which is integral; if $h:\mathrm{Z}\to\mathrm{Y}$ is the structural morphism, on has $h(z')=y'$.
It suffices to prove that there exists in $f^{-1}(y')$ a generisation $x'$ of $x$ such that the image of $x'$ by the morphism $g_{y'}:f^{-1}(y')\to h^{-1}(y')$ equals $z'$.
Moreover $z'$ is also the generic point of $h^{-1}(y')=\operatorname{Spec}(k(y')[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_r])$; as the morphism $g_{y'}$ is not ramified, hence quasi-finite \sref{IV.17.4.2}, it follows from \sref{IV.5.4.1} that the restriction of $g_{y'}$ to every irreducible component of \emph{dimension} $\geq r$ of $f^{-1}(y')$ is a dominant morphism.
Now, there exists by hypothesis a component containing a generisation $x'$ of $x$; \emph{a fortiori} its generic point is a generisation of $x$, whence the conclusion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Application to Noetherian local algebras complete over a field}
\label{subsection:IV.18.11}

The following lemma generalises \sref[0]{0.21.9.1} and \sref[0]{0.21.9.2}:

\begin{lemma}[18.11.1]
\label{IV.18.11.1}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{A}$ a Noetherian complete local ring which is a $k$-algebra, $\mathrm{K}$ the residue field of $\mathrm{A}$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item If the $\mathrm{K}$-vector space $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{K}/k}$ is of finite rank, the $\mathrm{A}$-module $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}$ is of finite type.
  \item If moreover $\mathrm{A}$ is a $k$-algebra formally smooth (for the adic topology), $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}$ is a free $\mathrm{A}$-module of rank equal to
\[
  \dim(\mathrm{A})+\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{K}}(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{K}/k})-\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{K}}(\Upsilon_{\mathrm{K}/k}).
\]
\end{enumerate}
Furthermore, for every subfield $k_0$ of $k$ such that $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{K}/k_0}$ is a $k$-vector space of finite rank, $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k_0}$ is a free $\mathrm{A}$-module of rank equal to
\[
  \dim(\mathrm{A})+\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{K}}(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{K}/k_0})-\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{K}}(\Upsilon_{\mathrm{K}/k_0})+\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{K}}(\Omega^1_{k/k_0}).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The assertion (i) is noted as a reminder, having been proved in \sref[0]{0.20.7.15}.
To prove (ii), note that this assertion has been in fact established by the demonstration of \sref[0]{0.21.9.2}, making only intervene the finiteness of the transcendence degree of $\mathrm{K}$ over $k$ (by way of the Cartier equality \sref[0]{0.21.7.1}).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[18.11.2]
\label{IV.18.11.2}
Let $k$ be a field of characteristic $p>0$, $\mathrm{C}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian, complete, whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$, and which is integral; let $n$ be its dimension, $\mathrm{L}$ its field of fractions.
Then $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}/k}$ and $\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}/k}$ are $\mathrm{L}$-vector spaces of finite rank, and one has
\begin{equation}
\label{IV.18.11.2.1}
\tag{18.11.2.1}
  \operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}}(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}/k})-\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}}(\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}/k})\geq n.
\end{equation}
\oldpage[IV-4]{170}
Suppose furthermore that $[k:k^p]<+\infty$.
Then one has the equality
\begin{equation}
\label{IV.18.11.2.2}
\tag{18.11.2.2}
  \operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}}(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}/k})-\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}}(\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}/k})=n.
\end{equation}
Furthermore, $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{C}/k}$ is then isomorphic to $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{C}/k}$, hence \sref{IV.18.11.1} is a $\mathrm{C}$-module of finite type.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We know in fact \sref[0]{0.19.8.9} that there exists a sub-$k$-algebra $\mathrm{C}_0$ of $\mathrm{C}$ isomorphic to a formal power series algebra in $n$ variables $k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]$ and such that $\mathrm{C}$ is a $\mathrm{C}_0$-algebra finite.
Hence, $\mathrm{L}$ is a \emph{finite} extension of the field of fractions $\mathrm{L}_0=k((\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n))$ of $\mathrm{C}_0$.
Now, one has the exact sequence of $\mathrm{L}$-vector spaces obtained by applying \sref[0]{0.20.6.17.1} to the three fields $k\subset\mathrm{L}_0\subset\mathrm{L}$ (and taking into account \sref[0]{0.20.4.13}):
\[
  0\to\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{L}_0}\mathrm{L}\to\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}/k}\to\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}/\mathrm{L}_0}\to\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{L}_0}\mathrm{L}\to\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}/k}\to\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}/\mathrm{L}_0}\to 0.
\]
Since $\mathrm{L}$ is a finite extension of $\mathrm{L}_0$, $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}/\mathrm{L}_0}$ and $\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}/\mathrm{L}_0}$ are $\mathrm{L}$-vector spaces of finite rank having the same rank, by the Cartier equality \sref[0]{0.21.7.1}; from the preceding exact sequence we therefore already deduce that
\[
  \operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}}(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}/k})-\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}}(\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}/k})=\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}_0}(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}_0/k})-\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}_0}(\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}_0/k}).
\]

To prove \sref{IV.18.11.2.1} or \sref{IV.18.11.2.2}, one can therefore restrict to proving these relations by replacing $\mathrm{C}$ and $\mathrm{L}$ by $\mathrm{C}_0$ and $\mathrm{L}_0$.
As $\mathrm{L}_0$ is separable over $k$ \sref[0]{0.21.7.1}, we have $\Upsilon_{\mathrm{L}_0/k}=0$ \sref[0]{0.20.6.3}; one deduces hence already that in all cases $\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}_0}(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{L}_0/k})\geq n$, with equality when $[k:k^p]<+\infty$; this already proves \sref{IV.18.11.2.1} and \sref{IV.18.11.2.2}.

Finally, to see that $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{C}/k}$ is isomorphic to $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{C}/k}$ when $[k:k^p]<+\infty$, it suffices to prove that $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{C}/k}$ is a $\mathrm{C}$-module of finite type, since $\mathrm{C}$ is a Noetherian complete local ring \sref[0]{0.7.3.5}, taking into account \sref[0]{0.20.4.5}.
Since $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{C}/k}$ is isomorphic to $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{C}/k}$ and that $k[\mathrm{C}_0^p]\subset k[\mathrm{C}^p]$, the result follows that $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{C}/k}$ is a $\mathrm{C}$-module of finite type and $\mathrm{C}_0$ a $k[\mathrm{C}_0^p]$-module of finite type in the hypothesis $[k:k^p]<+\infty$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[18.11.3]
\label{IV.18.11.3}
Let $\mathrm{A}$ be a Noetherian local ring, $\mathfrak{p}$ a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$ such that $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$ is a Cohen-Macaulay ring.
For every system of parameters $(t_i)_{1\leq i\leq s}$ of $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$, there exists a sequence $(x_i)_{1\leq i\leq s}$ of elements of $\mathfrak{p}$ such that the $x_i$ form part of a system of parameters of $\mathrm{A}$ \oldpage[IV-4]{172}and that, for all $i$, $x_i/1$ is congruent to $t_i$ modulo the ideal $t_1\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}+\ldots+t_{i-1}\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$.
In particular, if $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$ is regular and if the $t_i$ form a regular system of parameters of $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$, so it is also the case of the $x_i/1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This lemma itself will be a consequence of the following:
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[18.11.3.4]
\label{IV.18.11.3.4}
Let $\mathrm{A}$ be a Noetherian local ring, $\mathfrak{p}$ a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$, $x$ an element of $\mathrm{A}$; then there exists an element $x'\in\mathrm{A}$ such that $x'/1=x/1$ in $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$ and that $x'$ does not belong to any of the minimal prime ideals of $\mathrm{A}$ not contained in $\mathfrak{p}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let us first show how \sref{IV.18.11.3.4} implies \sref{IV.18.11.3}.
Multiplying the $t_i$ if necessary by invertible elements of $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$, one can suppose that they are of the form $x_i/1$, with $x_i\in\mathfrak{p}$ for $1\leq i\leq s$.
We reason by induction on $s$.
As $t_1$ forms part of a system of parameters of $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$ \sref[0]{0.16.5.5}, there is no $x_1'\in\mathrm{A}$ such that $x_1'/1=t_1$ that can belong to a minimal prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$, hence it cannot belong to any of the minimal prime ideals of $\mathrm{A}$ contained in $\mathfrak{p}$.
By virtue of \sref{IV.18.11.3.4}, one can moreover choose $x_1'$ such that $x_1'/1=t_1$ (hence $x_1'\in\mathfrak{p}$) and that $x_1'$ does not belong to \emph{any} minimal prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$, hence $x_1'$ forms part of a system of parameters of $\mathrm{A}$ \sref[0]{0.16.3.4} and \sref{IV.16.3.7}.
One reasons then by induction, considering the ring $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{A}/x_1'\mathrm{A}$ and the prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}'=\mathfrak{p}/x_1'\mathrm{A}$; as $\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}'}'\cong\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}/t_1\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$, $\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}'}'$ is also a Cohen-Macaulay ring \sref[0]{0.16.5.5}, and the images $t_i'$ ($2\leq i\leq s$) of the $t_i$ in $\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}'}'$ form a system of parameters of this ring; it suffices to apply $\mathrm{A}'$ and to the $t_i'$ ($2\leq i\leq s$) the induction hypothesis.
The last assertion of \sref{IV.18.11.3} follows from the fact that in $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$, a system of parameters that generates the maximal ideal is a regular system of parameters \sref[0]{0.17.1.1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \sref{IV.18.11.3.4}]
Let $(p_k')_{1\leq k\leq r}$ be the suite of minimal prime ideals of $\mathrm{A}$ not contained in $\mathfrak{p}$, and let $(\mathfrak{p}_j)_{1\leq j\leq n}$ be the suite of other minimal prime ideals of $\mathrm{A}$; one can suppose that $r\geq 1$.
As $\mathfrak{p}_k'$ does not contain $\bigcap_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathfrak{p}_j$, there exists an element $y\in\bigcap_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathfrak{p}_j$ that does not belong to any of the $\mathfrak{p}_k'$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~II, \textsection~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~1, prop.~2); moreover $y/1$ belongs to all the minimal prime ideals of $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$, hence is nilpotent; if $(y/1)^h=0$, the element $x'=x+y^h$ will satisfy the requirement, since $x'/1=x/1$ by definition of the $\mathfrak{p}_k'$, on has $x'\notin\mathfrak{p}_k'$ for $1\leq k\leq r$, and on the other hand, if $\mathfrak{p}_j$ is a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$ not contained in $\mathfrak{p}$ but such that $x\notin\mathfrak{p}_j$, we also have $x'\notin\mathfrak{p}_j$ since $y\in\mathfrak{p}_j$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of {\sref{IV.18.11.3}}, continued]
We return to the proof of \sref{IV.18.11.3} when $p=1$.
By virtue of \sref{IV.18.11.3.4}, there exists a regular system of parameters $(t_i)_{1\leq i\leq s}$ of $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$ where the $x_i$ ($1\leq i\leq s$) belong to $\mathfrak{p}$ and form part of a system of parameters $(x_j)_{1\leq j\leq n}$ of $\mathrm{A}$.
Set $\mathrm{A}_0=k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]$; as $\mathrm{A}$ is a $k$-algebra complete and the $x_j$ belong to the maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}$ of $\mathrm{A}$, there exists a local $k$-homomorphism $u:\mathrm{A}_0\to\mathrm{A}$ such that $u(\mathrm{T}_j)=x_j$ for $1\leq j\leq n$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~III, \textsection~4, n\textsuperscript{o}~5, prop.~6); if $n$ is the ideal of $\mathrm{A}$ generated by the $x_j$ ($1\leq j\leq n$), it is by hypothesis an ideal of definition of $\mathrm{A}$; one deduces therefore from \sref[0]{0.7.4.4} and \sref{IV.7.4.3} that $u$ makes $\mathrm{A}$ an $\mathrm{A}_0$-algebra \emph{finite}.

Set $\mathfrak{p}_0=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\mathrm{A}_0\mathrm{T}_j$, $\mathrm{B}_0=(\mathrm{A}_0)_{\mathfrak{p}_0}$ and $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_0}\mathrm{B}_0$, so that $u_{\mathfrak{p}_0}:\mathrm{B}_0\to\mathrm{B}$ makes
\oldpage[IV-4]{173}
$\mathrm{B}$ a $\mathrm{B}_0$-algebra finite; moreover, if $\mathfrak{p}'$ is the ideal of $\mathrm{B}$ generated by $\mathfrak{p}$, on has $\mathrm{B}_{\mathfrak{p}'}=\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$; as $\mathfrak{p}'$ contains $\mathfrak{p}_0\mathrm{B}_0$ by construction, it is above the maximal ideal $\mathfrak{p}_0\mathrm{B}_0$ of $\mathrm{B}_0$.
Let us show that the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}_0)$ is \emph{not ramified} at the point $\mathfrak{p}'$: indeed this follows from the fact that $k(\mathfrak{p}')$ is a finite extension of the field $k(\mathfrak{p}_0)$ of characteristic $0$, hence is \emph{necessarily separable}, and that $\mathrm{B}_{\mathfrak{p}'}/\mathfrak{p}_0\mathrm{B}_{\mathfrak{p}'}=k(\mathfrak{p}')$ by virtue of the choice of the $x_i$ for $1\leq j\leq s$ \sref{IV.17.4.1}.

Let us now note the following lemma:

\begin{lemma}[18.11.3.5]
\label{IV.18.11.3.5}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{R}$, $\mathrm{S}$ two Noetherian complete local $k$-algebras, such that, if $\mathrm{K}$ is the residue field of $\mathrm{S}$, $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{K}/k}$ is of finite rank over $\mathrm{K}$; let $u:\mathrm{R}\to\mathrm{S}$ be a $k$-homomorphism making $\mathrm{S}$ an $\mathrm{R}$-algebra finite; then $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{S}/\mathrm{R}}=\Omega^1_{\mathrm{S}/\mathrm{R}}$, and the sequence
\begin{equation}
\label{IV.18.11.3.6}
\tag{18.11.3.6}
  \hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{R}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{R}}\mathrm{S}\xrightarrow{v}\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{S}/k}\xrightarrow{w}\Omega^1_{\mathrm{S}/\mathrm{R}}\to 0
\end{equation}
(cf.\ \sref[0]{0.20.7.17.3}) is exact.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The first assertion follows from the fact that $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{S}/\mathrm{R}}$ is an $\mathrm{S}$-module of finite type \sref[0]{0.20.4.7} and that $w$ is surjective; but it follows from \sref{IV.18.11.1} that $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{S}/k}$ is an $\mathrm{S}$-module of finite type; on concludes that every sub-$\mathrm{S}$-module of $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{S}/k}$ is closed \sref[0]{0.7.3.5}, whence the lemma.
\end{proof}

Applying this lemma to the case where $\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{A}_0$, $\mathrm{S}=\mathrm{A}$, and noting that $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}_0/k}$ is an $\mathrm{A}_0$-module free of rank $n$ \sref[0]{0.21.9.3}; hence on a $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}_0/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_0}\mathrm{A}=\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}_0/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_0}\mathrm{A}$ and this is an $\mathrm{A}$-module free of rank $n$.
Since $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is not ramified on $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}_0)$ at the point $\mathfrak{p}'$, on a $(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}/\mathrm{A}_0})_\mathfrak{p}=\Omega^1_{\mathrm{B}_{\mathfrak{p}'}/\mathrm{B}_0}=0$ \sref{IV.16.4.15} and \sref{IV.17.4.1}.
If one localises the exact sequence \sref{IV.18.11.3.6} (applied to $\mathrm{A}_0$ and $\mathrm{A}$) at $\mathfrak{p}$, one obtains therefore a surjective homomorphism $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}_0/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_0}\mathrm{A})_\mathfrak{p}\to(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$, and the conclusion follows that $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}$ is a $\mathrm{B}$-module of rank $n$, and the ring being complete and integral by the Cohen theorem \sref[0]{0.19.8.8},~(ii)) and the fact that the field of fractions of $\mathrm{A}$ is of characteristic $0$.
Hence the $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ admits a system of $n$ generators.
But the $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}$ is of \emph{rank} $n$, by virtue of \sref[0]{0.21.9.5}, which is applicable to the ring being complete and integral by the Cohen theorem \sref[0]{0.19.8.8},~(ii)) and the fact that the field of fractions of $\mathrm{A}$ is of characteristic $0$.
Hence the $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ admits also a system of $n$ generators, and since it is of rank $n$, this quotient is necessarily \emph{free}, whence the conclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[18.11.4]
\label{IV.18.11.4}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{A}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian and complete, whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$.
Let $k'$ be an arbitrary extension of $k$, and set $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{A}\widehat{\otimes}_kk'$.
Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item $\mathrm{A}'$ is a semi-local Noetherian ring, complete, composed as a direct product of complete local rings $\mathrm{A}_i'$ ($1\leq i\leq r$) which are $\mathrm{A}$-modules faithfully flat, and whose residue fields are finite extensions of $k'$; if $\mathfrak{m}$ is the maximal ideal of $\mathrm{A}$, $\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{A}'$ is an ideal of definition of $\mathrm{A}'$; and $\dim(\mathrm{A}_i')=\dim(\mathrm{A})$ for all $i$.
  \item For all $i$, $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}$ is canonically isomorphic to $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}_i'$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\oldpage[IV-4]{174}
The first assertions follow immediately from \sref{IV.7.5.5} and \sref[0]{0.6.6.2}; the fact that $\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{A}'$ is an ideal of definition of $\mathrm{A}'$ also follows from \sref{IV.7.5.5}, since $\mathrm{K}$ is the residue field of $\mathrm{A}$, $\mathrm{K}\otimes_k k'$ is a finite $k'$-algebra.
Finally $\mathrm{A}_i'/\mathfrak{m}\mathrm{A}_i'$ is one of the Artinian local rings whose direct product is $\mathrm{A}'\otimes_k k'$; hence as $\mathrm{A}_i'$ is an $\mathrm{A}$-module flat, the equality of dimensions of $\mathrm{A}$ and $\mathrm{A}_i'$ follows from \sref{IV.6.1.2}.

(ii) As $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}$ is, by the hypothesis on $\mathrm{K}$, an $\mathrm{A}$-module of finite type \sref{IV.18.11.1}, $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'$ is complete and identifies with the completed tensor product $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'$; by virtue of the associativity of the completed tensor product \sref[0]{0.7.7.4}, $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'=\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{A}\widehat{\otimes}_kk')$ identifies with $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\widehat{\otimes}_kk'$.
But $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\otimes_kk'$ identifies with $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}''/k'}$, where $\mathrm{A}''=\mathrm{A}\otimes_kk'$ \sref[0]{0.20.5.5}, and as $\mathrm{A}'$ is by definition the separated completion of $\mathrm{A}''$, the separated completion $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}''/k'}$ identifies by construction to $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}'/k'}$ \sref[0]{0.20.7.4}; furthermore, if $\mathfrak{r}$ is the radical of $\mathrm{A}'$, the $\mathfrak{r}$-preadique topology on $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}'/k'}$ identifies with the product of the $\mathfrak{m}_i'$-preadique topologies on the $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}_i'/k'}$ (where $\mathfrak{m}_i'$ is the maximal ideal of $\mathrm{A}_i'$); finally, it follows that the separated complete $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}'/k'}$ for the $\mathfrak{r}$-preadique topology identifies with the product of the separated completions $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}_i'/k'}$ for the $\mathfrak{m}_i'$-preadique topologies, and it suffices to use \sref[0]{0.20.4.5}.

The conclusion of (ii) follows now from the fact that $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}'/k'}$ is a direct sum of the $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}_i'/k'}$ \sref[0]{0.20.4.13}, and if $\mathfrak{r}$ is the radical of $\mathrm{A}'$, the $\mathfrak{r}$-preadique topology on $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}'/k'}$ identifies with the product of the $\mathfrak{m}_i'$-preadique topologies on the $\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}_i'/k'}$ (where $\mathfrak{m}_i'$ is the maximal ideal of $\mathrm{A}_i'$); the separated complete $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}'/k'}$ for the $\mathfrak{r}$-preadique topology identifies therefore with the product of the separated completions $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}_i'/k'}$ for the $\mathfrak{m}_i'$-preadique topologies, hence one has a canonical isomorphism
\[
  \hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}'/k'}\xrightarrow{\sim}\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{A}'.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[18.11.5]
\label{IV.18.11.5}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{A}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian and complete, whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$, $\mathfrak{p}$ a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$ distinct from the maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}$, such that there exists a unique minimal prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}\subset\mathfrak{p}$ for which $\dim(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{q})=\dim(\mathrm{A})$ (which will in particular hold when $\mathrm{A}$ is equidimensional), $m$ an integer $\geq 0$.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\alph*)]
  \item The $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ admits a system of $m$ generators.
  \item There exists a local $k$-homomorphism $u:\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{A}$, where $\mathrm{B}=k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_m]]$, making $\mathrm{A}$ a $\mathrm{B}$-algebra finite, and such that the corresponding morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is \'etale at the point $\mathfrak{p}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
To prove that $b)$ implies $a)$, let us note that by virtue of the lemma \sref{IV.18.11.3.5}, on has the exact sequence
\[
  \hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{B}/k}\otimes_\mathrm{B}\mathrm{A}\to\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\to\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}/\mathrm{B}}\to 0
\]
since $\mathrm{A}$ is a $\mathrm{B}$-algebra finite; localising at $\mathfrak{p}$ and noting that by hypothesis $(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}/\mathrm{B}})_\mathfrak{p}=0$ \sref{IV.17.4.1}, one obtains a surjective homomorphism $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{B}/k}\otimes_\mathrm{B}\mathrm{A})_\mathfrak{p}\to(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$, and
\oldpage[IV-4]{175}
the conclusion follows that $\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{B}/k}$ is a $\mathrm{B}$-module of rank $m$ \sref[0]{0.21.9.3}.

To prove that $a)$ implies $b)$, let us first prove the following lemmas.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[18.11.5.1]
\label{IV.18.11.5.1}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{A}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian and complete, whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$, $\mathfrak{p}$ a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$, $\mathfrak{q}$ a minimal prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$ contained in $\mathfrak{p}$.
Then the minimum number of generators of the $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ is at least equal to $\dim(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{q})$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Set $n=\dim(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{q})$, and let $m$ be the minimum number of generators of the $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$, which is equal to $\operatorname{rg}_{k(\mathfrak{p})}((\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}}k(\mathfrak{p}))$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~II, \textsection~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~2, cor.~2 of prop.~4).
Note that $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{q}=(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}}\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{q}$, hence the minimum number of generators of the $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{q}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{q}$ is \emph{at most} equal to $m$.
It suffices therefore to consider the case where $\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{q}$ is minimal.
In second place, let us show one can restrict to the case where $k$ is \emph{algebraically closed}.
Indeed, let $k'$ be an algebraic closure of $k$, and set $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{A}\widehat{\otimes}_kk'$, which is composed as a direct product of $k'$-local algebras $\mathrm{A}_i'$ ($1\leq i\leq r$), whose residue fields are isomorphic to $k'$, and which are $\mathrm{A}$-modules faithfully flat \sref{IV.18.11.4}.
Applying \sref{IV.2.3.4} and \sref{IV.6.1.1}, one sees that in a given $\mathrm{A}_i'$, there exists a minimal prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}_i'$ above $\mathfrak{q}$ such that $n'=\dim(\mathrm{A}_i'/\mathfrak{q}_i')\geq\dim(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{q})=n$.
On the other hand by \sref{IV.18.11.4}, the minimum $m'$ of generators of the $(\mathrm{A}_i')_{\mathfrak{q}_i'}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}_i'/k'})_{\mathfrak{q}_i'}$ is \emph{at most} equal to $m$; whence our assertion.

Suppose therefore $k$ algebraically closed and $\mathfrak{q}=0$.
In the case where $\mathfrak{q}=0$, one can moreover restrict to the case where $\mathrm{A}$ is integral and $\mathrm{K}$ separable over the algebraically closed field $k$, hence $\mathrm{A}$ is a geometrically regular algebra \sref{IV.6.7.6}; one can therefore apply \sref{IV.18.11.3} for $\mathfrak{p}=0$ since $k=k^p$ and in this case $m=n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[18.11.5.2]
\label{IV.18.11.5.2}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{A}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian and complete, whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$, $\mathfrak{m}$ its maximal ideal, $\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{m}$ a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$.
Let $m$ be the minimum number of generators of the $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$, and let $(x_i)_{1\leq i\leq m}$ be a family of elements of $\mathfrak{m}$ not belonging to $\mathfrak{p}$.
Then there exist $m$ invertible elements $u_i$ ($1\leq i\leq m$) of $\mathrm{A}$ such that if $y_i=u_ix_i$, the canonical images of the $dy_i$ in $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ generate this $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For all $x\in\mathrm{A}$, denote by $\delta(x)$ the canonical image of $dx$ $(=d_{\mathrm{A}/k}x)$ in $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}}k(\mathfrak{p})=\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}k(\mathfrak{p})$; as this is a $k(\mathfrak{p})$-vector space of dimension $m$ by hypothesis, it suffices (by virtue of the Nakayama lemma) to prove that one can determine the $u_i$ such that the $\delta(u_ix_i)$ form a free system.
We reason by induction and suppose for an integer $r<m$, we have determined the $u_i$ ($1\leq i\leq r$) such that the $\delta(u_ix_i)$ for $1\leq i\leq r$ are linearly independent over $k(\mathfrak{p})$; if $\delta(x_{r+1})$ is not a linear combination of the $\delta(u_ix_i)$ for $1\leq i\leq r$, one takes $u_{r+1}=1$ to continue the induction.
In the contrary case, note that for $u\in\mathrm{A}$, $\delta(ux_{r+1})$ is the canonical image of $(du)x_{r+1}+u(dx_{r+1})$; as $u(dx_{r+1})$ has a canonical image which is a linear combination
\oldpage[IV-4]{176}
of the $\delta(u_ix_i)$ for $1\leq i\leq r$, there exists therefore $z\in\mathrm{A}$ such that $\delta(z)$ is not a linear combination of the $\delta(u_ix_i)$ for $1\leq i\leq r$; if $z\notin\mathfrak{m}$, one takes $u=z$; otherwise, $1+z$ is invertible and $d(1+z)=d(z)$ since $(du)x_{r+1}=\delta(z)$; one takes then $u=1+z$, which finishes the proof of \sref{IV.18.11.5.2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of {\sref{IV.18.11.5}}, continued]
We now return to the proof of the implication $a)\Rightarrow b)$ in \sref{IV.18.11.5}.
Let us first note that since $\mathfrak{p}\neq\mathfrak{m}$, there exists a system of parameters $(x_i)_{1\leq i\leq n}$ of $\mathrm{A}$ (with $n=\dim(\mathrm{A})$) such that $x_i\notin\mathfrak{p}$ for all $i$, without which $\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{p}$ would be of finite length, and $\mathfrak{p}$ would be maximal, contrary to the hypothesis.
But if for example $x_1\notin\mathfrak{p}$, it suffices, for each index $i$ such that $x_i\in\mathfrak{p}$, to replace $x_i$ by $x_1+x_i$, to have a system of parameters none of whose elements belong to $\mathfrak{p}$.
The relation $\dim(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{q})=n$ implies, by virtue of \sref{IV.18.11.5.1}, that $m\geq n$; one can therefore consider a family $(x_i)_{1\leq i\leq m}$ of elements $x_i\notin\mathfrak{p}$, whose $n$ first form a system of parameters of $\mathrm{A}$.
Multiplying furthermore the $x_i$ by invertible elements $u_i$ of $\mathrm{A}$, one can, by \sref{IV.18.11.5.2}, suppose that the images of $dx_i$ ($1\leq i\leq m$) in $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ generate this $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module, and the multiplication by the $u_i$ has not altered the fact that the $x_i$ for $1\leq i\leq n$ form a system of parameters.
We shall consider the local $k$-homomorphism $u:\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{A}$ such that $u(\mathrm{T}_i)=x_i$ for $1\leq i\leq m$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~III, \textsection~4, n\textsuperscript{o}~5, prop.~6); as the $x_i$ generate an ideal of definition of $\mathrm{A}$, it follows from \sref[0]{0.7.4.4} and \sref{IV.7.4.3} that $u$ makes $\mathrm{A}$ a $\mathrm{B}$-algebra \emph{finite}.

But the $d_\mathrm{A}x_i$ are the canonical images by $v$ of the elements $d_\mathrm{B}\mathrm{T}_i\otimes 1$ \sref[0]{0.20.5.2.6}.
If one localises the exact sequence preceding at $\mathfrak{p}$, we see therefore that $v_\mathfrak{p}:(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{B}/k}\otimes_\mathrm{B}\mathrm{A})_\mathfrak{p}\to(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ is \emph{surjective}, and consequently $0=(\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}/\mathrm{B}})_\mathfrak{p}=\Omega^1_{\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}/\mathrm{B}_\mathfrak{r}}$ (where $\mathfrak{r}$ is the inverse image of $\mathfrak{p}$ in $\mathrm{A}$, cf.\ \sref{IV.16.4.15}), thus the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is not ramified at $\mathfrak{p}$ by \sref{IV.17.4.1}, and the property $b)$ of \sref{IV.18.11.5} follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[18.11.6]
\label{IV.18.11.6}
In the statement of \sref{IV.18.11.5}, one can drop the hypothesis $\mathfrak{p}\neq\mathfrak{m}$.
Indeed, for every local $k$-homomorphism $u:\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{A}$, where $\mathrm{B}=k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_r]]$ ($r$ any integer) making $\mathrm{A}$ a $\mathrm{B}$-algebra finite, $\mathfrak{m}$ is the only point of $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ above the maximal ideal $\mathfrak{n}$ of $\mathrm{B}$; the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ cannot be unramified at $\mathfrak{m}$ unless $\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{n}\mathrm{A}$ is a field, an extension separable of $k$ \sref{IV.17.4.1}, which implies that the residue field $\mathrm{K}$ of $\mathrm{A}$ is a separable extension of $k$.
If this condition is not satisfied, the conclusion of \sref{IV.18.11.5} can never be satisfied for $\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}$, whatever the integer $m$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[18.11.7]
\label{IV.18.11.7}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{A}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian and complete, equidimensional, whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$, $\mathfrak{p}$ a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$.
Then the minimum number of generators of the $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ is at least equal to $\dim(\mathrm{A})$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
It suffices to set $\mathfrak{p}=0$ in \sref{IV.18.11.7}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.11.8]
\label{IV.18.11.8}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{A}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian and complete, integral, whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$.
If $\mathrm{K}$ is the field of fractions of $\mathrm{A}$, one has $\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{K}}((\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{K})\geq\dim(\mathrm{A})$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
It suffices to set $\mathfrak{p}=0$ in \sref{IV.18.11.7}.
\end{proof}

\oldpage[IV-4]{177}
\begin{corollary}[18.11.9]
\label{IV.18.11.9}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{A}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian and complete, of dimension $n$, whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$.
Let $\mathfrak{p}$ be a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$ distinct from the maximal ideal, and containing a minimal prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}$ such that $\dim(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{q})=n$.
Suppose that the $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ admits $n$ generators.
Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item The $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ is free of rank $n$.
  \item There exists a local $k$-homomorphism $u:\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{A}$, where $\mathrm{B}=k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]$, making $\mathrm{A}$ a $\mathrm{B}$-algebra finite, and such that the corresponding morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is \'etale at the point $\mathfrak{p}$.
  \item The $k$-algebra $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$ is geometrically regular over $k$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let us prove first of all (ii); by virtue of \sref{IV.18.11.5}, there exists a local $k$-homomorphism $u:\mathrm{B}=k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]\to\mathrm{A}$ making $\mathrm{A}$ a $\mathrm{B}$-algebra finite, and such that the corresponding morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is not ramified at the point $\mathfrak{p}$; set $\mathfrak{r}=u^{-1}(\mathfrak{p})$.
The hypothesis on $\mathfrak{q}\subset\mathfrak{p}$ and the fact that $\mathrm{A}$ is a quotient ring of a regular ring \sref[0]{0.19.8.8} imply that $\dim(\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p})=n-\dim(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{p})$ \sref[0]{0.16.5.12}.
Similarly $\dim(\mathrm{B}_\mathfrak{r})=n-\dim(\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{r})$.
Finally, since the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is not ramified at the point $\mathfrak{p}$, the fibre of this morphism at the point $\mathfrak{r}$ is of dimension $0$, hence \sref[0]{0.16.3.9} $\dim(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{p})\leq\dim(\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{r})$ and consequently $\dim(\mathrm{B}_\mathfrak{r})\leq\dim(\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p})$.
We conclude therefore from \sref{IV.18.10.1} that the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is \'etale at $\mathfrak{p}$.

The assertion (iii) follows from the fact that $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$ is formally smooth and that $\mathrm{B}_\mathfrak{r}$ is formally smooth on $k$ for their preadique topologies \sref[0]{0.19.3.4} and \sref{IV.19.3.5}), hence $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$ is formally smooth on $k$ for its preadique topology, and consequently geometrically regular over $k$ \sref[0]{0.22.5.8}.

Let us finally prove (i).
Let $k'$ be an algebraically closed extension of $k$, and let us consider the $k'$-algebra semi-local $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{A}\widehat{\otimes}_kk'$.
As a $\mathrm{B}$-module of finite type via $u$, $\mathrm{A}$ is considered as a $\mathrm{B}$-algebra finite; since the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is finite and \'etale at every point $\mathfrak{p}'$ above $\mathfrak{p}$ \sref{IV.17.3.3}; furthermore, $\mathrm{A}'$ is composed as a direct product of local rings $\mathrm{A}_i'$ of dimension $n$ \sref{IV.18.11.4} and $\mathfrak{p}'$ identifies with a prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}_i'$ of one of the $\mathrm{A}_i'$.
The same reasoning as above proves that $(\mathrm{A}_i')_{\mathfrak{p}_i'}$ is geometrically regular over $k'$, and as $k'$ is perfect, the fact $d)$ implies $b)$, hence by faithful flatness \sref{IV.18.11.4} and \sref{IV.2.5.2}, $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ is an $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module free of finite type.
But $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}_i'/k'})_{\mathfrak{p}_i'}=(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{A}_i')_{\mathfrak{p}_i'}$ \sref{IV.18.11.4} and $(\mathrm{A}_i')_{\mathfrak{p}_i'}$ is a faithfully flat $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module; one sees that $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ is an $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module \emph{free} of finite type \sref{IV.2.5.2}; this finishes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[18.11.10]
\label{IV.18.11.10}
\oldpage[IV-4]{178}
Let $k$ be a field of characteristic exponent $p$, $\mathrm{A}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian and complete, whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$, $\mathfrak{p}$ a prime ideal of $\mathrm{A}$ distinct from the maximal ideal.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\alph*)]
  \item For every extension $k'$ of $k$, and every prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}'$ of $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{A}\widehat{\otimes}_kk'$ above $\mathfrak{p}$, $\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}'}$ is a regular ring.
\item[a$'$)] There exists a perfect extension $k'$ of $k$ and a prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}'$ of $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{A}\widehat{\otimes}_kk'$ above $\mathfrak{p}$, such that $\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}'}$ is regular.
  \item Let $n$ be the largest of the dimensions of the irreducible components of $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ to which $\mathfrak{p}$ belongs; then $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ is an $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module free of rank $n$.
\end{enumerate}
Suppose furthermore that $n=\dim(\mathrm{A})$ (which will be the case if $\mathrm{A}$ is equidimensional); then the preceding conditions are also equivalent to:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\alph*)]
  \setcounter{enumi}{2}
  \item There exists a local $k$-homomorphism $u:\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{A}$, where $\mathrm{B}=k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]$, making $\mathrm{A}$ a $\mathrm{B}$-algebra finite, and such that the corresponding morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is \'etale at the point $\mathfrak{p}$.
  \item The ring $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$ is geometrically regular over $k$.
\end{enumerate}
Furthermore, if $[k:k^p]<+\infty$, the condition $d)$ is equivalent to $a')$, $b)$, and $c)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The fact that $d)$ implies $b)$ when $[k:k^p]<+\infty$ is nothing other than \sref{IV.18.11.3}.
It is clear that $a)$ implies $a')$.
Let us show $c)$ implies $a)$ when $\dim(\mathrm{A})=n$.
One says that $\mathrm{A}$ is an $\mathrm{A}$-module flat \sref{IV.18.11.4} and $\dim(\mathrm{A}')=\dim(\mathrm{A})=n$; it follows from \sref{IV.2.3.4} and \sref{IV.6.1.1} that there exists a minimal prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}'$ of $\mathrm{A}'$ contained in $\mathfrak{p}'$, above $\mathfrak{q}$ and such that $\dim(\mathrm{A}'/\mathfrak{q}')=\dim(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{q})=n$.
Furthermore, by \sref{IV.18.11.4}, $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}'/k'})_{\mathfrak{p}'}=(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}}\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}'}'; $ as $k'$ is perfect, the hypothesis implies that it is geometrically regular over $k'$, one can apply to $\mathrm{A}'$ and $\mathfrak{p}'$ the fact $d)$ implies $b)$, hence $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}'/k'})_{\mathfrak{p}'}$ is an $\mathrm{A}_{\mathfrak{p}'}$-module free of rank $n$; by faithful flatness \sref{IV.18.11.4} and \sref{IV.2.5.2}, $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ is an $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module free of rank $n$.
This shows the equivalence of $a)$, $a')$, $b)$, and $c)$ when $\dim(\mathrm{A})=n$.
It remains to prove that $a)$, $a')$, and $b)$ are still equivalent in the general case.
Let $\mathfrak{J}$ be the kernel of $\mathrm{A}$, the noyau of the canonical homomorphism $\mathrm{A}\to\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$, and set $\mathrm{A}_1=\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{J}$; we necessarily have $\mathfrak{J}\subset\mathfrak{p}$, and if we set $\mathfrak{p}_1=\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{J}$, the canonical homomorphism $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}\to(\mathrm{A}_1)_{\mathfrak{p}_1}$ is \emph{bijective}; it follows from \sref[I]{I.6.5.4} that the canonical injection $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A}_1)\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ is an \emph{isomorphism local} at the point $\mathfrak{p}_1$, and one has $\mathfrak{J}_\mathfrak{p}=0$.
One sees as in \sref{IV.18.11.3} that the sequence is exact, and that one concludes that the condition $a)$ (resp.\ $a')$)) for the ring $\mathrm{A}$ and the prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$ is \emph{equivalent} to the condition $a)$ (resp.\ $a')$)) for the ring $\mathrm{A}_1$ and the prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}_1$.
Now, all the minimal prime ideals of $\mathrm{A}$ contained in $\mathfrak{p}$ contain $\mathfrak{J}$ since $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A}_1)\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ is an isomorphism local at $\mathfrak{p}_1$; on the other hand the $\mathrm{Ass}_\mathrm{A}(\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{J})$ are the prime ideals of $\mathrm{Ass}(\mathrm{A})$ which are contained in $\mathfrak{p}$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~IV, \textsection~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~2, prop.~6); hence all the minimal prime ideals of $\mathrm{A}_1$ are contained in $\mathfrak{p}_1$, and consequently $\dim(\mathrm{A}_1)=n$.
It suffices then to apply to $\mathrm{A}_1$ and $\mathfrak{p}_1$ which has been proved above.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[18.11.11]
\label{IV.18.11.11}
\oldpage[IV-4]{179}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item The equivalence of conditions $d)$ and $b)$ in \sref{IV.18.11.10} is no longer valid when one no longer supposes that $[k:k^p]<+\infty$.
Indeed, by the example of \sref[0]{0.22.7.7},~(ii)), the ring $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{q}$ is integral and of dimension $1$; on the other hand, the sequence
\[
  (\mathfrak{q}/\mathfrak{q}^2)\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{L}\xrightarrow{j}\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{L}\to\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{B}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{L}\to 0
\]
is exact (same demonstration as in \sref{IV.18.11.3}), and we know that $j$ \emph{is not injective}, hence $j=0$ since $(\mathfrak{q}/\mathfrak{q}^2)\otimes_\mathrm{A}\mathrm{L}$ is of rank $1$.
We conclude that $\operatorname{rg}_{\mathrm{L}}(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{B}/k}\otimes_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{L})=2$; as $\mathrm{B}_\mathfrak{p}$ is a $k$-algebra geometrically regular, one sees that the condition $d)$ does not imply here the condition $b)$.
  \item With the notations of \sref{IV.18.11.10} and supposing that $n=\dim(\mathrm{A})$ and $(x_i)_{1\leq i\leq n}$ a system of parameters of $\mathrm{A}$ not belonging to $\mathfrak{p}$; one deduces a local $k$-homomorphism $u:\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{A}$ such that $u(\mathrm{T}_i)=x_i$ for $1\leq i\leq n$, making $\mathrm{A}$ a $\mathrm{B}$-algebra finite.
For the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ to be \emph{\'etale} at the point $\mathfrak{p}$, it is necessary and sufficient that the images of $d_\mathrm{A}x_i$ in $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ form a \emph{system of generators} of this $\mathrm{A}_\mathfrak{p}$-module.
Indeed, as one has seen in the proof of \sref{IV.18.11.5} that if the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is not ramified at $\mathfrak{p}$, the canonical homomorphism $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{B}/k}\otimes_\mathrm{B}\mathrm{A})_\mathfrak{p}\to(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{A}/k})_\mathfrak{p}$ is surjective and the images of the elements $d_\mathrm{B}\mathrm{T}_i\otimes 1$, which generate $(\hat{\Omega}^1_{\mathrm{B}/k}\otimes_\mathrm{B}\mathrm{A})_\mathfrak{p}$, are the $d_\mathrm{A}x_i$; whence the \emph{necessity} of the condition.
Conversely, the same reasoning shows that if this condition is satisfied, the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is not ramified at $\mathfrak{p}$, and the reasoning of \sref{IV.18.11.9} proves in fact that this morphism is \'etale at $\mathfrak{p}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[18.11.12]
\label{IV.18.11.12}
Let $k$ be a field of characteristic exponent $p$ such that $[k:k^p]<+\infty$, $\mathrm{A}$ a $k$-algebra, local Noetherian and complete and integral, which is not a field, and whose residue field is a finite extension of $k$.
Then there exists a finite radicial extension $k'$ of $k$ such that, setting $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{A}\otimes_kk'$, the $k'$-algebra $\mathrm{A}'_{\mathrm{red}}$ and the prime ideal $\mathfrak{o}$ of this algebra satisfy the equivalent conditions $a)$, $a')$, $b)$, $c)$, and $d)$ of \sref{IV.18.11.10}.
In particular, if $n=\dim(\mathrm{A})=\dim(\mathrm{A}')$,
\oldpage[IV-4]{180}
there exists a $k'$-homomorphism local $\mathrm{B}'=k'[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]\to\mathrm{A}'_\mathrm{red}$ making $\mathrm{A}'_\mathrm{red}$ a $\mathrm{B}'$-algebra finite, and such that the field of fractions $\mathrm{K}'$ of $\mathrm{A}'_\mathrm{red}$ is a finite separable extension of the field of fractions $k'((\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n))$ of $\mathrm{B}'$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A}')\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ being complete, radicial, and finite, $\mathrm{A}'$ is a local ring, complete and its nilradical is the only prime ideal above $\mathfrak{o}$ of $\mathrm{A}$; by flatness, $\mathrm{A}'$ identifies with a sub-ring of $\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk'$, which is also the total ring of fractions of $\mathrm{A}'$; on has therefore $\mathrm{K}'=(\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk')_{\mathrm{red}}$.
It is a question of proving, by the equivalence $d)\Leftrightarrow c)$, that there exists a finite radicial extension $k'$ of $k$ such that $(\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk')_\mathrm{red}$ is a separable extension of $k'$.
Or one knows \sref[0]{0.19.8.9} that, under all the hypotheses made, there exists a sub-$k$-algebra $\mathrm{C}=k[[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]]$ of $\mathrm{A}$ such that $\mathrm{A}$ is a finite $\mathrm{C}$-algebra; hence, $\mathrm{K}$ is a \emph{finite} extension of the field of fractions $\mathrm{K}_1=k((\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n))$ of $\mathrm{C}$, which is \emph{separable} on $k$ \sref[0]{0.21.9.6.4}.
If $p$ is the characteristic exponent of $k$, one can therefore write $\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}}=\mathrm{K}\otimes_{k_1}(\mathrm{K}_1\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}})$ and $\mathrm{K}_1\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}}$ is a \emph{field}, a radicial extension of $\mathrm{K}_1$; on concludes that $\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}}$ is a finite algebra over the field $\mathrm{K}_1\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}}$, hence is \emph{Artinian}.
The conclusion follows therefore from the following more general lemma:
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[18.11.12.1]
\label{IV.18.11.12.1}
Let $k$ be a field of characteristic $p>0$, $\mathrm{K}$ an extension of $k$.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\alph*)]
  \item The ring $\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}}$ is Artinian.
  \item There exists an extension $k'$ of $k$ of finite type such that $(\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk')_{\mathrm{red}}$ is a $k'$-algebra separable.
  \item There exists a finite radicial extension $k'$ of $k$ such that $(\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk')_\mathrm{red}$ is a separable extension of $k'$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let us first show that $c)$ implies $a)$.
The ring $\mathrm{A}=\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk'$ is then a local Artinian ring, and if $\mathfrak{N}$ is nilradical, the residue field $\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{A}/\mathfrak{N}$ is separable over $k'$; hence $\mathrm{L}\otimes_{k'}k^{p^{-\infty}}$ is separable over $k'$, and as it is equal to $(\mathrm{A}\otimes_{k'}k^{p^{-\infty}})/(\mathfrak{N}\otimes_{k'}k^{p^{-\infty}})$, one sees that $\mathfrak{N}\otimes_{k'}k^{p^{-\infty}}$ is the nilradical of $\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}}$; as $\mathfrak{N}$ is a nilpotent ideal of finite type, the nilradical of $\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}}$ is of finite type, hence the ring $\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}}$ is Artinian.

Conversely, let us prove that $a)$ implies $c)$.
Let $\mathfrak{N}$ be the nilradical of the local Artinian ring $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk^{p^{-\infty}}$, which is by hypothesis generated by a finite number $d$ of elements of the form $y_i=\sum_j x_{ij}\otimes\xi_{ij}$, $x_{ij}\in\mathrm{K}$, $\xi_{ij}\in k^{p^{-\infty}}$.
Let $k'$ be the finite radicial extension of $k$ generated by the $\xi_{ij}$, $\mathfrak{N}_0$ the ideal of $\mathrm{B}_0=\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk'$ generated by the $y_i$; it is clear that $\mathfrak{N}_0\otimes_{k'}k^{p^{-\infty}}=\mathfrak{N}$, and hence $\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathrm{B}_0=\mathfrak{N}_0$, and so it is equal to $\mathrm{B}_0/\mathfrak{N}_0=(\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk')_{\mathrm{red}}$ is separable over $k$ \sref{IV.4.6.1}.

It is clear that $c)$ implies $b)$.
Conversely, suppose $b)$ is satisfied, and let us note that there exists a separable extension $k_1$ of $k$ such that $k'$ is a finite radicial extension of $k_1$; set $\mathrm{K}_1=\mathrm{K}\otimes_kk_1$, which is a field.
Applying the equivalence of $a)$ and $c)$ to the extension $\mathrm{K}_1$,
\oldpage[IV-4]{181}
on sees that $\mathrm{K}_1\otimes_{k_1}k_1^{p^{-\infty}}$ is also Artinian (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~I, \textsection~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~5, cor.\ of prop.~8); we have thus shown that $b)$ implies $a)$, which finishes the proof.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Applications of \'etale localisation to quasi-finite morphisms}
\label{subsection:IV.18.12}

The results of this section were communicated by P.~Deligne.

\begin{theorem}[18.12.1]
\label{IV.18.12.1}
Let $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ be a locally of finite type morphism, $x$ a point of $\mathrm{X}$, $y=f(x)$.
Suppose that $x$ is an isolated point of the space $f^{-1}(y)$.
Then there exist an \'etale morphism $\mathrm{Y}'\to\mathrm{Y}$, a point $x'$ of $\mathrm{X}'=\mathrm{X}\times_{\mathrm{Y}}\mathrm{Y}'$ above $x$ and a neighbourhood $\mathrm{V}'$ of $x'$ in $\mathrm{X}'$ such that, if $f'=f_{(\mathrm{Y}')}:\mathrm{X}'\to\mathrm{Y}'$, $f'|\mathrm{V}'$ is a finite morphism.
Furthermore $\mathrm{V}'$ is both open and closed, and $\mathrm{X}'$ is therefore the sum of two sub-preschemes induced on open subsets of $\mathrm{X}'$, of which one is finite on $\mathrm{Y}'$ and contains $x'$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The last assertion follows from the fact that, if $f$ is separated, it is the same for $f'$; hence, if $j':\mathrm{V}'\to\mathrm{X}'$ is the injection canonical, and the fact that $f'$ is finite implies that it is the same for $j'$ \sref[II]{II.6.1.5}, and $\mathrm{V}'=j'(\mathrm{V}')$ is closed in $\mathrm{X}'$ \sref[II]{II.6.1.10}.

The question being local on $\mathrm{X}$, one can suppose $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ and $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ affine, $\mathrm{B}$ being an $\mathrm{A}$-algebra of finite type, and one can then suppose $f^{-1}(y)=\{x\}$.
Hence the question is local on $\mathrm{Y}$, hence of the form $\mathrm{C}/\mathfrak{J}$, where $\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_r]$ and $\mathfrak{J}$ is an ideal of type finite of $\mathrm{E}$; we then have $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{E}'/\mathfrak{J}\mathrm{E}'$, where $\mathrm{E}'=\mathrm{C}'[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_r]$.
Let $(\mathfrak{J}_\lambda)$ be the family of ideals of type fine of $\mathrm{C}$ contained in $\mathfrak{J}$, and denote the corresponding $\mathrm{X}_\lambda=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{C}/\mathfrak{J}_\lambda)$ considered as closed sub-preschemes of $\mathrm{Z}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{C})$, so that $\mathfrak{J}$ is the filtered union of the $\mathfrak{J}_\lambda$.
If $\mathrm{X}$ and the $\mathrm{X}_\lambda=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{C}/\mathfrak{J}_\lambda)$ are considered as closed sub-preschemes, $\mathrm{X}=\bigcap\mathrm{X}_\lambda$; if $f_\lambda:\mathrm{X}_\lambda\to\mathrm{Y}$ is the structural morphism, and as the sets $f_\lambda^{-1}(y)$ are closed in the Noetherian space $\mathrm{Z}_y=\operatorname{Spec}(k(y)[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_r])$, there exists an index $\lambda$ such that $f_\lambda^{-1}(y)=f^{-1}(y)=\{x\}$.
One can then suppose that for a given $\mathrm{X}_\lambda$, the condition holds that $\mathrm{X}$ is at point $x$; if one proves the proposition for such an $\mathrm{X}_\lambda$, it will follow for $\mathrm{X}$, since $x'$ is a point above $x$ in $\mathrm{Z}'=\mathrm{Z}\times_\mathrm{Y}\mathrm{Y}'$, $\mathrm{X}_\lambda'=p^{-1}(\mathrm{X}_\lambda)$, where $p:\mathrm{Z}'\to\mathrm{Z}$ is the projection (from the restriction of $f_\lambda$ to $\mathrm{X}_\lambda$), the same will hold for the open set $\mathrm{V}_\lambda'$ of $x'$ in $\mathrm{X}_\lambda'$ which will be finite over $\mathrm{Y}'$.
Furthermore $\mathrm{V}'$ and $\mathrm{X}'$ are sums of two open sub-preschemes, $\mathrm{V}'$ and $\mathrm{X}_\lambda'$; one answers the question by taking $\mathrm{V}'$ as a sum of $\mathrm{U}'$ and $\mathrm{V}'$.
In particular, note that the set of isolated points of $\mathrm{X}$ in their fibre et open in $\mathrm{X}$ \sref{IV.13.1.4}, hence $f$ is \emph{quasi-finite}.
Set $\mathrm{A}''=\sh{O}_{Y,y}$, and set $\mathrm{Y}''=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A}'')$.
Set $\mathrm{X}''=\mathrm{X}\times_{\mathrm{Y}}\mathrm{Y}''$, $f''=f_{(\mathrm{Y}'')}:\mathrm{X}''\to\mathrm{Y}''$; $\mathrm{X}''\to\mathrm{Y}''$ is \emph{separated}, quasi-finite and of presentation finite; $\mathrm{A}''$ is henselian, for every $x''\in\mathrm{X}''$ above $x$ an open and closed neighbourhood $\mathrm{V}''$ of $x''$ in $\mathrm{X}''$ which is finite on $\mathrm{Y}''$ \sref{IV.18.5.11},~$c$)); as the immersion $\mathrm{V}''\to\mathrm{X}''$ is open and closed, it is also quasi-compact, hence $f''|\mathrm{V}''$ is of presentation finite since $\mathrm{V}''\to\mathrm{X}''$ is open, and $\mathrm{X}''\to\mathrm{Y}''$ is of presentation finite.
$f''$ is finite and quasi-compact over $\mathrm{Y}''$ \sref{IV.1.6.2}, hence $f''|\mathrm{V}''$ is of presentation finite.

Since, by definition, $\mathrm{A}''$ is the inductive limit of a filtered inductive family $(\mathrm{A}_\lambda)$ of $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$-algebras strictly \'etales (\sref{IV.18.6.5}) and \sref{IV.8.1.2},~$a$))); also $\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{M}_\lambda}$ can be assumed to be a $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$-algebra of presentation finite, and it follows from \sref{IV.18.6.1} and \sref{IV.8.1.2},~$a$)) and the result of \sref{IV.17.7.8} that $\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{M}_\lambda}$ is a $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$-algebra \'etale for all $\lambda$; Namely, $\mathrm{Y}''$ is the inductive limit projective of a family $(Y_\alpha)$ of affine $\mathrm{Y}$-schemes \'etale.
If $x_\alpha''$ is the projection of $x''$ on $\mathrm{X}_\alpha'=\mathrm{X}\times_{\mathrm{Y}}\mathrm{Y}_\alpha$, on can suppose that $\mathrm{X}_\alpha'\cap f'^{-1}(\mathrm{U})$ where $\mathrm{V}_\alpha'$ is a neighbourhood of $x_\alpha''$ in $\mathrm{X}_\alpha'$, is finite on $\mathrm{Y}_\alpha$ \sref{IV.8.2.11}.
Finally, $\mathrm{V}''$ being of presentation finite over $\mathrm{Y}''$, on concludes from \sref{IV.8.10.5},~(x)) that for a suitable $\alpha$, $\mathrm{V}_\alpha'$ is finite over $\mathrm{Y}_\alpha'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[18.12.2]
\label{IV.18.12.2}
In the preceding proof, one sees (taking into account \sref{IV.18.6.2}) that one has constructed $\mathrm{Y}'$ answering the question, and in addition such that if $y'=f'(\mathrm{X}')$, the homomorphism $k(y)\to k(y')$ is bijective.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[18.12.3]
\label{IV.18.12.3}
Let $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ be a locally of finite type morphism and separated, $y$ a point of $\mathrm{Y}$ such that the underlying space $f^{-1}(y)$ is finite and discrete; in particular, a proper and quasi-finite morphism.
Then there exist an \'etale morphism $\mathrm{Y}'\to\mathrm{Y}$, a point $y'\in\mathrm{Y}'$ above $y$ such that $k(y')=k(y)$, and a decomposition of $\mathrm{X}'=\mathrm{X}\times_{\mathrm{Y}}\mathrm{Y}'$ as a sum of two sub-preschemes $\mathrm{X}_1'$, $\mathrm{X}_2'$ induced on open subsets of $\mathrm{X}'$, such that the restriction $f'=f_{(\mathrm{Y}')}:\mathrm{X}_1'\to\mathrm{Y}'$ is a finite morphism and that $\mathrm{X}_2'\cap f'^{-1}(y')=0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\oldpage[IV-4]{182}
Indeed, one can apply the corollary \sref{IV.18.12.3} at any point whatsoever $y$ of $\mathrm{Y}$.
Since $f'$ is a closed morphism, hence, since $\mathrm{X}_2'$ is closed in $\mathrm{X}'$, $f'(\mathrm{X}_2')$ is closed in $\mathrm{Y}'$ and does not contain $y'$; hence there exists by construction an open neighbourhood $\mathrm{U}'$ of $y'$ in $\mathrm{Y}'$ such that $f'^{-1}(\mathrm{U}')=\mathrm{X}\times_\mathrm{Y}\mathrm{U}'$ is finite on $\mathrm{Y}'$, and the morphism $\mathrm{U}'\to\mathrm{Y}$ is \'etale; the morphism $f'^{-1}(\mathrm{U}')\to\mathrm{U}'$ is faithfully flat and quasi-compact, hence the conclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.12.4]
\label{IV.18.12.4}
Let $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ be a finite morphism, it is necessary and sufficient that it be separated, universally closed and, for all $y\in\mathrm{Y}$, $f^{-1}(y)$ is a finite and discrete space; in particular, a proper and quasi-finite morphism.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, one can apply the corollary \sref{IV.18.12.3} at any point whatsoever $y$ of $\mathrm{Y}$.
Since $f'$ is a closed morphism, hence, since $\mathrm{X}_2'$ is closed in $\mathrm{X}'$, $f'(\mathrm{X}_2')$ is closed in $\mathrm{Y}'$ and does not contain $y'$; hence there exists by construction an open neighbourhood $\mathrm{U}'$ of $y'$ in $\mathrm{Y}'$ such that $f'^{-1}(\mathrm{U}')=\mathrm{X}\times_\mathrm{Y}\mathrm{U}'$ is finite over $\mathrm{Y}'$, and the morphism $\mathrm{U}'\to\mathrm{Y}$ is \'etale; the morphism $\mathrm{U}'\to\mathrm{Y}$ is faithfully flat and quasi-compact, hence by \sref{IV.2.7.1},~(xv)), the restriction of $f$ to $\mathrm{X}_\lambda$, being a finite morphism \sref[II]{II.6.1.10}, hence the conclusion results from \sref{IV.11.3.1}, since $f$ restricted is finite and quasi-compact.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[18.12.5]
\label{IV.18.12.5}
One notes that in the proof of \sref{IV.18.12.4} one has not used the hypothesis \sref{IV.18.12.4} in its general form, but only that $f_{(\mathrm{Y}')}$ is a universally closed morphism for every \'etale morphism $\mathrm{Y}'\to\mathrm{Y}$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[18.12.6]
\label{IV.18.12.6}
Let $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ be a morphism of preschemes.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\alph*)]
  \item $f$ is a closed immersion.
  \item $f$ is a proper monomorphism.
  \item $f$ is proper and, for all $y\in\mathrm{Y}$, the $k(y)$-prescheme $\mathrm{X}_y=f^{-1}(y)$ is radicial and geometrically reduced on $k(y)$ (i.e.\ empty or $k(y)$-isomorphic to $\operatorname{Spec}(k(y))$).
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
This is deduced from \sref{IV.18.12.4} as \sref{IV.18.11.5} is deduced from \sref{IV.8.11.1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[18.12.7]
\label{IV.18.12.7}
Let $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ be a locally of finite type morphism, $y$ a point of $\mathrm{Y}$.
For there to exist a neighbourhood $\mathrm{U}$ of $y$ such that the restriction $f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})\to\mathrm{U}$ of $f$ is a closed immersion, it is necessary and sufficient that $\mathrm{X}_y$ be a $k(y)$-prescheme radicial and geometrically reduced on $k(y)$ (i.e.\ empty or $k(y)$-isomorphic to $\operatorname{Spec}(k(y))$) and that there exist a neighbourhood $\mathrm{V}$ of $y$ such that the restriction $f^{-1}(\mathrm{V})\to\mathrm{V}$ of $f$ is a universally closed morphism.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The conditions being evidently necessary, let us prove that they are sufficient.
One can, by the second condition, already suppose $f$ universally closed.
Let us show, moreover, hence separated; this follows from the following lemma:
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[18.12.7.1]
\label{IV.18.12.7.1}
Let $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ be a closed morphism, $y\in\mathrm{Y}$ a point such that every neighbourhood of $\mathrm{X}_y$ contains an affine neighbourhood of $\mathrm{X}_y$ (condition always satisfied when $\mathrm{X}_y$ is empty or reduced to a single point).
Then there exists a neighbourhood $\mathrm{U}$ of $y$ such that the restriction $f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})\to\mathrm{U}$ of $f$ is an affine morphism.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, let $\mathrm{U}_0$ be an affine open neighbourhood of $y$ in $\mathrm{Y}$ and let $\mathrm{V}$ be a neighbourhood of $\mathrm{X}_y$ contained in $f^{-1}(\mathrm{U}_0)$ such that $f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})\subset\mathrm{V}$, which is affine.
As $f$ is closed and $\mathrm{V}$ open, there exists a neighbourhood $\mathrm{U}\subset\mathrm{U}_0$ of $y$ such that $f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})\subset\mathrm{V}$.
As the restriction $f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})\to\mathrm{U}$ is also an affine morphism \sref[II]{II.1.2.5}.
\end{proof}

Suppose therefore $f$ affine and universally closed, and let us show that for all $y\in\mathrm{Y}$ such that the restriction $f^{-1}(\mathrm{U})\to\mathrm{U}$ of $f$ is a closed immersion, it suffices to prove that $f$ is quasi-finite \sref{IV.18.12.4}, and since $\mathrm{X}_y$ is empty or reduced to a single point, $f$ is quasi-finite \sref{IV.18.12.4}, taking into account \sref{IV.18.12.6}.

\begin{proposition}[18.12.8]
\label{IV.18.12.8}
Let $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ be a morphism of preschemes.
For $f$ to be entire, it is necessary and sufficient that $f$ be affine and universally closed.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We can suppose $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$, and it suffices to prove that every element $b\in\mathrm{B}$ is entire on $\mathrm{A}$ \sref[II]{II.6.1.1}.
Let $\mathrm{B}'$ be the sub-$\mathrm{A}$-algebra of $\mathrm{B}$ generated by $b$, which is an $\mathrm{A}$-algebra of finite type; set $\mathrm{X}'=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}')$, so that $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ factorises as $\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{g}\mathrm{X}'\xrightarrow{h}\mathrm{Y}$, where $h$ is of finite type, and $g$ is \emph{dominant} since the homomorphism $\mathrm{B}'\to\mathrm{B}$ is injective \sref[I]{I.1.2.7}.
As $h$ is separated and $h\circ g$ universally closed, $g$ is universally closed \sref[II]{II.5.4.3} and
\oldpage[IV-4]{183}
\sref{IV.5.4.9}); hence $h$ is also universally closed, therefore proper since it is of finite type and separated.
But then, for all $y\in\mathrm{Y}$, the morphism $h^{-1}(y)\to\operatorname{Spec}(k(y))$ deduced from $h$ is proper and affine, hence \emph{finite} \sref[III]{III.4.4.2}, in other words $h$ is \emph{quasi-finite}; one deduces therefore from \sref{IV.18.12.4} that $h$ is finite, and consequently $b$ is entire on $\mathrm{A}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[18.12.9]
\label{IV.18.12.9}
One can say that a morphism $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ is entire when one supposes only separated, universally closed, and such that for all $y\in\mathrm{Y}$, the morphism $f^{-1}(y)\to\operatorname{Spec}(k(y))$ deduced from $f$ is entire.
It would suffice to prove that these conditions imply that $f$ is affine, or again that every fibre $\mathrm{X}_y$ is contained in an affine open neighbourhood.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[18.12.10]
\label{IV.18.12.10}
A morphism $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ which is injective and universally closed is entire.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
It suffices, by \sref{IV.18.12.8}, to prove that $f$ is affine, which follows from the lemma \sref{IV.18.12.7.1} and the hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.12.11]
\label{IV.18.12.11}
Let $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ be a morphism of preschemes (resp.\ a locally of finite type morphism).
For $f$ to be a universal homeomorphism \sref{IV.2.4.2}, it is necessary and sufficient that $f$ be entire (resp.\ finite), radicial, and surjective.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
One knows that the conditions are sufficient \sref{IV.2.4.5}; they are necessary by virtue of \sref{IV.18.12.10} and \sref{IV.18.12.4}.
The last proposition improves \sref{IV.8.11.2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[18.12.12]
\label{IV.18.12.12}
Every morphism $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ which is quasi-finite and separated is quasi-affine.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Set $\mathscr{A}=f_*(\sh{O}_X)$, which is an $\sh{O}_Y$-Algebra quasi-coherent and separated \sref{IV.1.7.4}; and $\mathrm{Z}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{A})$ \sref[II]{II.1.3.1}, so that $f$ factorises canonically as $\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{g}\mathrm{Z}\xrightarrow{h}\mathrm{Y}$, $h$ being affine and $g$ corresponding to the automorphism identical to $\mathscr{A}$ \sref[II]{II.1.2.7}; it suffices to prove that $g$ is an \emph{open immersion}, or, what comes to the same \sref{IV.17.9.1}, that the morphism $g$ is \'etale and \emph{radicial}.
It suffices evidently to prove that, for all $y\in\mathrm{Y}$, the morphism $g$ is \'etale and radicial at each point of $f^{-1}(y)$.
One can apply the result to $\mathrm{X}$, $f$ the restriction and $\mathrm{Y}$ by $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{Y}$, and the sections of $f$ of $f$; hence $f$ is quasi-compact and separated, and the fact that $f$ is \'etale and radicial at each point of $f^{-1}(y)$ is part of \sref{IV.18.10.2},~(iii)), quasi-separable,  from $\mathrm{X}$, $f$, and $g$ as in \sref{IV.18.12.3}, whose $\mathrm{X}'$ is the extension $\mathrm{Z}'=\mathrm{Z}\times_{\mathrm{Y}}\mathrm{Y}'$ a $\sh{O}_{Y'}$-Module $\sh{O}_{X'}/\mathfrak{J}\mathrm{E}'$, so that as $\mathrm{X}$ and $\mathrm{Y}$ are both $\mathrm{Y}$-schemes \'etale \sref{IV.17.3.2}; hence by \sref[II]{II.1.2.7} $f$ factorises as $\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{g}\mathrm{Z}\xrightarrow{h}\mathrm{Y}$, where $g$ is the canonical injection from the identity on $\mathscr{A}$, and $g$ is an \emph{open immersion}, and $g$ is \emph{radicial}, whence the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.12.13]
\label{IV.18.12.13}
(``Main Theorem'' of Zariski).
Let $\mathrm{Y}$ be a prescheme quasi-compact and quasi-separated.
Then there exists a factorisation of $f$
\[
  \mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{g}\mathrm{Z}\xrightarrow{u}\mathrm{Y}
\]
where $g$ is an open immersion (necessarily quasi-compact) and $u$ a finite morphism.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, it follows from \sref{IV.18.12.12} that $f$ is quasi-affine, hence quasi-projective.
One can therefore apply \sref{IV.8.12.8}, where the hypothesis on $\sh{O}_Y$-Module ample can in fact be replaced by the sole hypothesis that $\mathrm{Y}$ is quasi-compact and quasi-separated: indeed, it follows from \sref{IV.8.12.3} that the existence of the factorisation announced is a \emph{local} property on $\mathrm{Y}$, and it suffices to prove it when $\mathrm{Y}$ is affine.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[18.12.14]
\label{IV.18.12.14}
One can give a proof of \sref{IV.18.12.13} analogous to that of \sref{IV.18.12.12} (but not using \sref{IV.8.12.3}, hence \sref{IV.18.5.11}), which itself uses the ``Main Theorem'' under its local form \sref{IV.8.12.9}).
Keeping indeed the notations of the proof of \sref{IV.18.12.12}, and let $\mathscr{B}$ be the $\sh{O}_Y$-Algebra quasi-coherente, integral closure of $\sh{O}_Y$ in $\mathscr{A}$ \sref[II]{II.6.3.2}.
If one sets $\mathrm{T}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathscr{B})$, it suffices, by virtue of \sref{IV.8.12.3}, to prove that $g:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{T}$ corresponding to the canonical injection $\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{A}$ is an immersion, and radicial.
With the notations of \sref{IV.18.12.12}, and one can suppose that $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{C})$ and $\mathrm{Y}'=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{C}')$ are affine, $\mathrm{C}'$ being a $\mathrm{C}$-algebra \'etale \sref{IV.17.3.2}; hence $\mathscr{A}=\overline{\mathrm{A}}$, where $\mathrm{A}$ is a $\mathrm{C}$-algebra, $\mathrm{B}$ the integral closure of $\mathrm{C}$ in $\mathrm{A}$.
The algebra $\mathrm{A}'$ is a $\mathrm{C}'$-algebra, $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{A}\otimes_\mathrm{C}\mathrm{C}'$, and $\mathscr{B}=\overline{\mathrm{B}}$, where $\mathrm{B}$ is the integral closure of $\mathrm{C}$ in $\mathrm{A}$.
As $\mathrm{Z}'=\mathrm{Z}\times_\mathrm{Y}\mathrm{Y}'$ and the $\mathrm{X}_\lambda'$ and $\mathrm{X}_\lambda$ are \'etale over $\mathrm{X}$, the conclusion follows in this case.

When $\mathrm{C}$ is Noetherian, the demonstration of \sref{IV.6.14.4} no longer requires the use of \sref{IV.6.14.1}.
Indeed, the reductions successives of the proof of \sref{IV.6.14.4} lead back, without using \sref{IV.6.14.1}, to the case where $\mathrm{C}$ is \emph{integral}, $\mathrm{A}$ the field of fractions of $\mathrm{C}$, and the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}')\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is \'etale, it follows from \sref{IV.17.5.7} that $\mathrm{B}'$ is a normal ring, and the reasoning terminates by making appeal only to the easy lemma \sref{IV.6.14.1}, and not to the difficult part of the proof of \sref{IV.6.14.1}.
\end{remark}

\oldpage[IV-4]{184}
\begin{proposition}[18.12.15]
\label{IV.18.12.15}
Let $\mathrm{G}$ be a ring, $\mathrm{C}'$ a $\mathrm{C}$-algebra \'etale, $\mathrm{A}$ a $\mathrm{C}$-algebra, $\mathrm{B}$ the integral closure of $\mathrm{C}$ in $\mathrm{A}$; on sets $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{A}\otimes_\mathrm{C}\mathrm{C}'$, $\mathrm{B}'=\mathrm{B}\otimes_\mathrm{C}\mathrm{C}'$, $\mathrm{B}'$ identifying to a sub-algebra of $\mathrm{A}'$; then $\mathrm{B}'$ is the integral closure of $\mathrm{C}'$ in $\mathrm{A}'$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Considering the filtered increasing family of sub-$\mathrm{C}$-algebras of type finite of $\mathrm{A}$ and using the fact that $\mathrm{A}$ is a $\mathrm{C}$-algebra of type fine, hence of the form $\mathrm{E}/\mathfrak{J}$, where $\mathrm{E}=\mathrm{C}[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_r]$ and $\mathfrak{J}$ is an ideal of type fine of $\mathrm{E}$; we then have $\mathrm{A}'=\mathrm{E}'/\mathfrak{J}\mathrm{E}'$, where $\mathrm{E}'=\mathrm{C}'[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_r]$.
Let $(\mathfrak{J}_\lambda)$ be the increasing filtered family of the ideals of type fine of $\mathrm{C}$ contained in $\mathfrak{J}$ and denote $\mathrm{B}_\lambda$ the integral closure of $\mathrm{C}$ in $\mathrm{E}/\mathfrak{J}_\lambda$; if $\mathrm{B}_\lambda$ is the inductive limit of the integral closures $\mathrm{B}_\lambda$, $\mathrm{B}$ is the inductive limit of the $\mathrm{B}_\lambda$, as one sees by the reasoning of \sref{IV.5.13.4}; same reasoning for $\mathrm{C}$ and the $\mathrm{B}_\lambda\otimes_\mathrm{C}\mathrm{C}'$ for $\beta\geq\alpha$.
The same, $\mathrm{A}'$ is the inductive limit of the integral closures of $\mathrm{C}'$ in $\mathrm{E}'/\mathfrak{J}_\lambda\mathrm{E}'$, hence the same reasoning as the preceding shows that $\mathrm{B}'=\mathrm{B}\otimes_\mathrm{C}\mathrm{C}'$ is the integral closure of $\mathrm{C}'$ in $\mathrm{A}'$ once we have reduced to the case where $\mathrm{C}$ is Noetherian, the proposition becomes a particular case of \sref{IV.6.14.4}.
It is necessary however to observe that, when $\mathrm{C}'$ is supposed \emph{\'etale} on $\mathrm{C}$ Noetherian, the demonstration of \sref{IV.6.14.4} no longer requires that one use \sref{IV.6.14.1}.
Indeed, the successive reductions in the proof of \sref{IV.6.14.4} lead back, \emph{without using} \sref{IV.6.14.1}, to the case where $\mathrm{C}$ is \emph{integral}, $\mathrm{A}$ the field of fractions of $\mathrm{C}$, hence $\mathrm{B}$ is a normal ring and the morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}')\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$ is \'etale, it follows from \sref{IV.17.5.7} that $\mathrm{B}'$ is a normal ring, and the reasoning terminates by making appeal only to the elementary lemma \sref{IV.6.14.1}, and not the difficult part of the proof of \sref{IV.6.14.1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[18.12.16]
\label{IV.18.12.16}
Let $g:\mathrm{Y}\to\mathrm{S}$ be a quasi-compact morphism, $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ a separated and quasi-finite morphism.
For every $\sh{O}_Y$-Module invertible $\mathscr{L}$, ample relative to $\mathrm{S}$ \sref[II]{II.4.6.1}, the $\sh{O}_X$-Module $f^*(\mathscr{L})$ is ample relative to $\mathrm{S}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, by virtue of \sref{IV.18.12.12}, the morphism $f$ is quasi-affine, hence \sref[II]{II.5.1.6} the $\sh{O}_X$-Module $\sh{O}_X$ is ample for $f$.
We deduce from \sref[II]{II.4.6.13},~(iii)) that $\sh{O}_X\otimes_{\sh{O}_X}f^*(\mathscr{L}^{\otimes n})=f^*(\mathscr{L}^{\otimes n})$ is ample relative to $\mathrm{S}$ for $n$ large enough.
But as $f^*(\mathscr{L}^{\otimes n})=(f^*(\mathscr{L}))^{\otimes n}$, this implies that $f^*(\mathscr{L})$ is ample relative to $\mathrm{S}$ \sref[II]{II.4.6.9},~(i)).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[18.12.17]
\label{IV.18.12.17}
Let $\mathrm{Z}$ be an affine scheme, $h:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Z}$ a morphism of finite type, $\mathscr{L}$ an $\sh{O}_X$-Module invertible.
With the notations of \sref[II]{II.4.5.2}, the conditions of \sref[II]{II.4.5.2} (which are equivalent to saying that $\mathscr{L}$ is ample) are also equivalent to each of the following:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\rm b$''$)] $h$ is separated, one has $\mathrm{G}(\varepsilon)=\mathrm{X}$ and the canonical morphism $u:\mathrm{G}(\varepsilon)\to\operatorname{Proj}(\mathrm{S})$ has finite and discrete fibres.
  \item[\rm b$'''$)] One has $\mathrm{G}(\varepsilon)=\mathrm{X}$ and the canonical morphism $u$ is radicial.
\end{enumerate}

If $\mathrm{Z}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$, the ring $\mathrm{S}$ is canonically equipped with a structure of graded $\mathrm{A}$-algebra, hence $h=g\circ u$.
Since every radicial morphism is separated \sref[I]{I.1.8.7.1}, $b''')$ implies $b'')$.
As the condition $b)$ of \sref[II]{II.4.5.2} implies evidently $b'')$, it remains to see that $b'')$ implies $\mathscr{L}$ ample.
Since $h=g\circ u$ is of finite type by hypothesis, $u$ is also quasi-finite and separated \sref[I]{I.6.3.4}, hence by the hypothesis $b'')$ implies that $u$ is quasi-finite and separated \sref[I]{I.5.5.1}.
To prove that $\mathscr{L}$ is ample, we shall now apply \sref{IV.18.12.16}.
Set $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Proj}(\mathrm{S})$.
Since $\mathrm{X}$ is quasi-compact, there exists an integer $n>0$ and a finite number of elements $f_j\in\mathrm{S}_n$ such that the inverse images $u^{-1}(\mathrm{D}_+(f_j))$ cover $\mathrm{X}$; one can therefore consider $u$ as a quasi-finite and separated morphism of $\mathrm{X}$ in the open $\mathrm{Y}'=\bigcup\mathrm{D}_+(f_j)$ of $\mathrm{Y}$.
Let us consider then the $\sh{O}_{Y'}$-Module invertible $\mathscr{L}'=\sh{O}_Y(n)\,|\,\mathrm{Y}'$ \sref[II]{II.2.5.8}; it is very ample relative to $\mathrm{Z}$ \sref[II]{II.4.4.3}, and consequently it is the same for $u^*(\mathscr{L}')$ \sref[II]{II.4.4.3}.

$\mathscr{L}'=\sh{O}_Y(n)\,|\,\mathrm{Y}'$ \sref[II]{II.2.5.8}; it is very ample relative to $\mathrm{Z}$ \sref[II]{II.4.4.3}, and as $u^*(\mathscr{L}')=\mathscr{L}^{\otimes n}$ by \sref[II]{II.4.4.3}, $\mathscr{L}^{\otimes n}$ is thus ample relative to $\mathrm{S}$ (this amounts to saying that it is ample \sref[II]{II.4.6.6}), and consequently the same is true of $\mathscr{L}$ \sref[II]{II.4.5.6},~(i)).

We leave it to the reader to state analogous conditions equivalent to those of \sref[II]{II.4.6.3} for the $\sh{O}_X$-Modules relatively ample.
\end{corollary}
